Let me look at the environment: check Rocq version, available libraries.

Let me think about the proof strategy. The language is STLC call-by-value with locally nameless binders. Need strong normalization for closed well-typed terms.

Standard approach: logical relation / reducibility candidates.

Define:
- `sn t := strongly_normalizing t` (already given).
- Define a "neutral" or "term value" notion. For CBV, the standard logical relation:
  - `R_Bool t := sn t /\ (t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false \/ (value t = false ... ))`. Hmm. Actually for strong normalization under CBV, a standard logical predicate is:
    - R_Bool(t) := SN(t) and t is either a value of bool (true/false) or a stuck neutral term... Hmm, but well-typed closed terms never get stuck unless they contain free variables. Actually with locally nameless and free variables, terms in the relation might contain fvars.

Let's think: We need to prove normalization for closed terms. The standard "strong normalization" logical relation:

Define `R_T(t)` for terms possibly with free variables, with a context interpretation for free variables. But since typing is done with context Gamma and free variables, we need to handle open terms.

Alternative approach: Since free variables are interpreted by arbitrary terms? Actually, for closed terms, we can use a "hereditarily strongly normalizing" logical relation on closed terms only, and for the abstraction case use the T_Abs rule with a cofinite list L: we need to show `R_{T1->T2}(tm_abs T1 t1)` given that for all x not in L, `R_{T2}(open t1 (fvar x))` ... but the body with a free variable is not closed. So we need to handle open terms with contexts.

Standard approach from Software Foundations' Stlc / norm proofs: Define a "term closure" relation: a term t is in the relation at type T if for all closing substitutions... Or use `Gamma |- t \in T` semantics where free variables are interpreted by values in the relation.

Standard approach: define `R T t` for open terms t by universally quantifying over closing substitutions? That's a common technique: define the logical relation on open terms via "t is in R_T under all substitutions that map fvar to related terms". But substitutions introduce trouble.

Alternative: Use a context-based logical relation: define `Gamma |= t \in T` where the interpretation of Gamma is a set of substitutions from fvars to values (well, terms in relation). Then fundamental theorem: if Gamma |- t \in T then for any sigma satisfying Gamma, sigma t is SN. Then for closed, empty |> t \in T, empty substitution, get SN of t. But substitution on locally nameless terms with fvar substitution is straightforward: define `subst_fvar : (atom -> tm) -> tm -> tm` recursively (no binder issue since fvar). Actually with locally nameless, fvar variables are free; substituting fvars is straightforward and no capture issue since bvars are bound separately... Wait, but opening with fvar in T_Abs means the body has an fvar in it. Substituting fvar x with a term possibly containing bvars? Hmm, the term substituted can contain bvars if we substitute under a binder? But since fvar substitution doesn't have capture issues w.r.t. bvars? Actually there could be: if the term u substituted for fvar x contains a bvar that is bound outside... no, when we substitute fvar x by u inside a term t, u is a closed term (or a term with no bvars). Since terms are locally nameless, a "term" substituted at fvar positions is typically closed w.r.t. bvars (lc_at 0). Actually, careful: u may contain bvars if u is not locally closed. But the values in the relation are locally closed? Values include abstractions whose locally_closed requirement is given in the value predicate: `v_abs` requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t), i.e., lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t) = lc_at 1 t. So all values are locally closed (v_true, v_false trivially closed). Good. So substituting closed values for fvars is safe.

But then the semantics of application: R_{T1->T2}(t) := forall u, R_{T1}(u) -> R_{T2}(t u) and SN(t)... and maybe also value/stuck condition for the bool base. Hmm.

Actually, a cleaner approach for strong normalization via logical relations with "neutral" terms:

For CBV, define:
- `R_Bool(t) := SN(t) /\ (t = true \/ t = false \/ ~ value t)`. Hmm, is that right? Consider a term t that is SN but is a lambda of type Bool — that can't happen for well-typed terms, but the relation must be closed under reduction etc. The standard CR for CBV: 
  - For base type: terms that are SN and either a value of base type or a neutral term (a term that is not a value and all its reducts are in R? Hmm).

Actually, let's think about what we need. The fundamental theorem needs to handle:
- T_Var: Gamma x = Some T. Then the term is fvar x. Under context interpretation, fvar x has type T and is a value. To prove R_T(fvar x), we need the relation to include fvar x (a neutral term).
- T_Abs: lambda : T1, t. R_{T1->T2} lambda requires SN(lambda) and that for all u in R_{T1}, R_{T2}(lambda u). lambda u --> open t u. So we need: if R_{T1}(u) then R_{T2}(open t u), and SN of the application follows.
- T_App: t1 t2 where t1 : T1->T2, t2 : T1. Then R_{T2}(t1 t2): need SN(t1 t2) and the "value/neutral" property.

Here's the thing: For the base type, the relation must be "closed under head-expansion" (anti-reduction) and contain all neutral terms whose immediate reducts are in R, plus values true/false.

I think a standard approach for CBV strong normalization uses the "sn + neutral or value" formulation:

Define inductively/recursively:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t /\ (t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false \/ ~ value t)

  end.
```

A bare lambda never steps. So SN(lambda) holds trivially! Because there's no step rule with a lambda as the left side. Great, so SN of abstractions is vacuous.

Let me think about what the fundamental theorem should say. Let's set up the "semantic typing" relation:

`Gamma |= t \in T` := forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) -> R T (subst sigma t)`.

Where sigma : atom -> tm maps fvars to terms, and R T (sigma x) for each free var.

For the variable case: Gamma x = Some T, then subst sigma (fvar x) = sigma x, and we have R T (sigma x) directly. Good.

For T_App: subst sigma (app t1 t2) = app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2). We need R T2 (app ...). We have R (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1) by IH, which gives SN and the application property. And R T1 (subst sigma t2). Apply. Good.

How to show? The application steps to open (subst sigma t1) u when locally_closed (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) and value u. Is that the only possible reduction? So R T2 (app (abs ...) u) needs to be proved by the "anti-reduction"/CR property: if a term is SN and all its one-step reducts are in R T2, then it's in R T2. That's a general "CR" lemma we should prove for all types: R T is closed under "all immediate reducts in R" plus SN. Let's define:

`R_closure` : forall T t, SN t -> (forall t', t --> t' -> R T t') -> R T t.

Is that true by induction on the SN derivation? Let's see for T = Bool: need SN t (given) and (t = true \/ t = false \/ ~value t). Let me reconsider. Standard approach: define R on terms via the "forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u)" and rely on a lemma that R T2 is closed under anti-reduction (backwards closure). Actually the typical proof for STLC SN:

Standard approach: prove a general "CR" property by defining R with an explicit "neutral or value" clause, and prove "R T is closed under backward reduction" (if t --> t' and R T t' and SN t then R T t) by induction on T, using SN induction.

Let's look at this from a well-known source: Software Foundations `Norm.v` proves normalization for STLC with CBV using a logical relation with `R T t := ...`? Let me recall. Hmm, I don't remember exactly. But this is the standard Tait-style reducibility for CBV. Let me think about whether the anti-reduction property holds with `R Bool t := SN t` (no value/neutral clause).

With R Bool = SN, the anti-reduction lemma: for all T, if SN t and (forall t', t --> t' -> R T t') then R T t. For Bool: SN t is given, done. - If t = abs and u value and abs locally closed: t u --> open body u. Hmm, but R T2 (t u) requires... but wait, in this case t is an abstraction, and "all reducts of t are in R (T1->T2)" is vacuous. Hmm, then we can't conclude anything about t's body.

So the general "R T t iff SN t and all reducts in R T" (with the forward direction being: if R T t then all reducts in R T) is not provable in this naive form, because of the value case where a value has no reducts but isn't necessarily "good".

The standard fix: the logical relation for CBV should be defined with explicit closedness/neutrality:

Alternative standard approach (used in e.g. "A logical relation for CBV STLC SN"): Define R as a *strongly normalizing set closed under expansion with values*. Standard reducibility candidates for call-by-value (e.g., in the "operational semantics" approach by Pitts) require `R` to be a set of terms (possibly open, but treated as closed) such that:
1. R ⊆ SN (all terms in R are SN).
2. If t ∈ R and t --> t', then t' ∈ R (forward closure).
3. If t is a "value" and all its "reducts" (well, values have no reducts)... Hmm.

Standard CR for CBV (from e.g. "Call-by-value is dual to call-by-name" or standard normalization proofs): A set S of terms is a "reducibility candidate" if:
- S ⊆ SN
- if t ∈ S and t --> t' then t' ∈ S
- (expansion) if t is a term all of whose reducts are in S and t is "not a value" ... hmm.

Let me recall the standard approach in e.g. Harper's PFPL or in Coq formalizations: For CBV, define the logical relation as:

```
R Bool t := SN t
R (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u)
```

and prove:
1. If R T t then SN t (built-in for arrow; for Bool it's SN).
2. If R T t and t --> t' then R T t'. (closure under reduction)
3. If R T t then R T (t' ) when t' --> t is NOT provable generally... but we need "expansion": if t is a term whose reducts are all in R T and t is not an abstraction? Hmm.

Wait, let's revisit. Where do we actually need expansion? In the T_App case we don't need it: given R (T1->T2) t1, R T1 t2, we directly get R T2 (t1 t2). In the T_Abs case we need, for all u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u). There, we can prove it via a "value expansion" lemma: if u ∈ R T1, then app (abs T1 body) u ∈ R T2 provided open body u ∈ R T2 and SN (app (abs body) u). Hmm, but actually we can prove more directly: app (abs body) u where u ∈ R T1 hence SN u; we do induction on SN u and SN (open body u)? Hmm, we get open body u ∈ R T2 from the IH of typing, for this specific u, not for reducts.

Lemma (value expansion): If value v, SN v? Actually abstractions are trivially SN; true/false are SN. Hmm.

Let me think from first principles and design a proof that works.

Approach: use a "semantic type" predicate with the following clauses, designed to make the anti-reduction lemma provable via SN induction, and with a notion that handles values.

Common approach for CBV SN (e.g., in "The Gamma framework"): The logical relation for values and terms separately:

Define two relations, on values `V T v` and on terms `R T t`:
- `R T t := SN t /\ forall t', t -->* t' -> (value t' -> V T t')`? That is, every reachable value is in V.

Hmm, alternatively, define `R T t := SN t /\ forall t', t --> t' ...`.

Let me think of the standard "Tait's method for CBV" definition:

For a type T, define the set of terms R_T:
- R_Bool := { t | SN t } (or terms that eventually reduce to true/false or get stuck? For closed well-typed, they can't get stuck; SN suffices since the theorem only wants SN).
- R_{T1->T2} := { t | SN t and for all u ∈ R_{T1}^{val}? ... }

Hmm, for CBV, the argument to a function must be a value when the beta rule fires. But if we quantify over all terms u ∈ R_{T1} (which are SN), then app t u steps via ST_App2 on u if u is not a value, and via beta when u is a value. Since R_{T1} includes non-values, the app (abs) u can take many steps evaluating u. So the clause forall u ∈ R_{T1}, app t u ∈ R_{T2} is natural and handles this automatically, provided we have the right lemmas.

Let's carefully attempt to prove:

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.
```

Hmm, but here's a subtlety: for T = Bool, R Bool t = SN t. Then the "anti-reduction" lemma for the T_App case when the result type is Bool: we need SN (t1 t2) given R (T1->T2) t1 and R T1 t2. We can prove SN (t1 t2) from SN t1 and SN t2? That's a general lemma: SN of application given SN of parts. Let's check: Is SN (t1 t2) provable from SN t1 and SN t2 for CBV STLC? Yes: standard "SN is closed under application": by induction on SN t1 and SN t2. Consider reducts of t1 t2:
- ST_AppAbs: t1 = abs, t2 = v value, reduct = open body v. Hmm! We need SN of open body v. We don't know anything about body from SN (abs body) — vacuously SN. So this lemma is FALSE for arbitrary terms: t1 = abs T (some non-SN body like Ω), v = true: SN (abs T Ω) holds vacuously, SN true holds, but (abs T Ω) true --> open Ω true = Ω which is not SN. So indeed SN t1 and SN t2 do not imply SN (t1 t2).

Right, so we need the relation to include information about the body of abstractions. This is why the arrow clause is "forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u)". For an abstraction t = abs T1 body, R (T1->T2) t requires forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (abs T1 body · u), which in turn requires SN of the application and further applications... This is a coinductive-ish requirement, but it works because the relation is defined by recursion on the type and the application reduces the type.

But then the question: how do we prove R T2 (app (abs body) u) given forall u' (open body u' ∈ R T2) or similar? Let's see:

Given u ∈ R T1, want R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u).

We know (from the typing IH, in the actual proof) R T2 (open body u) and R T2 (open body u') for all reducts u' of u? Hmm, the typing IH gives it for the specific u we choose (since IH holds for all x not in L and all substitutions). Actually we need R T2 (app (abs body) u); the app's reducts:
1. If u is a value and abs is locally closed: open body u ∈ R T2 (we have this from IH).
2. u --> u': app (abs body) u --> app (abs body) u' (ST_App2). We need R T2 (app (abs body) u'). By induction on SN u, we can get this if we know open body u' ∈ R T2 for reducts u'. Do we have that from IH? The IH from typing gives R T2 (open body u') for the same fixed u'? Actually the typing IH is: for all x not in L, for all substitutions sigma... it gives R T2 (open body u') for *any* u' ∈ R T1? No wait. Let's be careful.

The fundamental theorem: Gamma |- t \in T => for all sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) => R T (subst sigma t).

In T_Abs case: Gamma |- abs T1 t1 \in T1->T2 from (forall x not in L, x:T1;Gamma |- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2). To prove R (T1->T2) (subst sigma (abs T1 t1)) = R (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)), we need:
- SN (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)): vacuous, OK.
- forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).

Fix u with R T1 u. We want R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).

Take x fresh (not in L, not in domain of sigma, etc.). Define sigma' := sigma + {x := u}. Then sigma' satisfies (x:T1; Gamma): for x, R T1 (sigma' x) = R T1 u ✓; for y in Gamma, sigma' y = sigma y (if y ≠ x) and R (Gamma y) (sigma y) ✓.

By IH applied to (x:T1; Gamma) |- open t1 (fvar x) : T2 with sigma', we get R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).

Now, subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u, provided x does not appear in t1 (choose x fresh w.r.t. t1's fvars) and... Actually substitution of fvar x by u in open t1 (fvar x): open t1 (fvar x) replaces bvar 0 with fvar x. Then substitute all fvars: fvar x -> u. Since u is locally closed (is it? u ∈ R T1 — need to prove R T1 u implies locally_closed u? Values are locally closed, but u ranges over all terms in R T1, which may not be locally closed if R is SN for Bool? Hmm. For Bool, R Bool u = SN u. A term with free bvars like `tm_bvar 0`? SN (tm_bvar 0) holds vacuously? Check: bvar 0 doesn't step, so SN (bvar 0) holds. Then substituting u = bvar 0 into the body could capture! E.g. body = abs Bool (bvar 1)? Hmm, open t1 (fvar x) substitutes bvar 0 at level 0 and shifts under binders. Then substituting fvar x with bvar 0, the bvar 0 could be captured by an enclosing lambda in t1? Let's see: t1 = abs Bool (bvar 1). open t1 (fvar x) = abs Bool (open_rec 1 (fvar x) (bvar 1)) = abs Bool (fvar x). Then subst fvar x -> bvar 0 gives abs Bool (bvar 0), which is wrong—it captured the variable. But we can avoid this by requiring substitutions map to locally closed terms.

So let's define R only for locally closed terms? Or add a local-closedness requirement in the relation. Since we ultimately care about closed terms, and the semantic context should map fvars to closed terms, we can:
- Define sigma satisfying Gamma with sigma x locally closed and R T (sigma x).

Then when we substitute, we need the "open with a locally closed term" property. Locally closed terms: lc_at 0. If body's bvars are all < some level, opening with a closed term preserves closure.

I think the substitution semantics is the way. But actually, there's a simpler formulation using environments that avoids explicit substitution lemmas: define the "denotation" of a term in an environment `rho : atom -> tm` directly as a function that builds the term with substitution as it goes:

```
Fixpoint denot (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | bvar i => bvar i
  | fvar x => rho x
  | app t1 t2 => app (denot rho t1) (denot rho t2)
  | abs T t1 => abs T (denot rho t1)
  | ...
  end
```
This is exactly fvar substitution. Fine.

### Standard approach: reducibility candidates with "neutral" terms

For CBV, the standard logical relation (as in e.g. Girard's proof adapted, or in "Normalization by Evaluation") uses the notion of "term in R" defined as:
- R_Bool := { t | SN t } (for our purposes, since we only need SN and closed terms of Bool are either true/false or reduce; weak head normalization would also work but SN is what we want)
- R_{A->B} := { t | SN t ∧ ∀u. R_A(u) → R_B(t u) }

Actually, let me reconsider: maybe there's a simpler route to the theorem exploiting the specific setting: strong normalization of STLC CBV. We could use the standard "logical relation on values and computations" from Pitts:

Define simultaneously:
- `V T v` : values of type T are reducible
- `C T t` : computations (terms) of type T are reducible

with:
- `V Bool v := v = true \/ v = false` (or value + SN)
- `V (A->B) v := SN v ∧ ∀u. V A u → C B (v u)` — actually since v is a value (abstraction), v u's reducts...
- `C T t := SN t ∧ ∀t'. t -->* t' → value t' → V T t'` (every value reachable from t is in V).

This is the standard "closed reducibility" for CBV: C T t means t is SN and all its reachable values are in V T. Then:
- Closure: if t ∈ C T and t --> t', then t' ∈ C T (easy).
- Expansion-ish: to show t ∈ C T, show SN t and for each t --> t', t' ∈ C T.

For the fundamental theorem:
- T_App: t1 ∈ C (A->B), t2 ∈ C A, want t1 t2 ∈ C B.
  - SN: need SN (t1 t2). This requires: SN t1, and for the beta case, body relates. Hmm, the C relation for arrows includes ∀u. V A u → C B (v u) for values v. But t1 may not be a value. For SN (t1 t2) with t1 ∈ C (A->B) and t2 ∈ C A, we need SN of the app, which follows from t1's SN and t2's SN plus a "value expansion" fact for the body of abstractions reachable... This is where we need: if v is a value in C (A->B) and u ∈ C A, then v u ∈ C B (hence SN). For v an abstraction abs body: v u's reducts: for u value, open body u; for u --> u', v u'. And open body u must be in C B — which comes from the typing IH? Hmm, but in the fundamental theorem, when t1 = abs body, we don't necessarily have typing IH at hand for the body? We do: the term t1 = abs A body has a typing derivation, which came from T_Abs with the body typing for fresh x. So the fundamental theorem applied to the premised typing of the body gives us open body u ∈ R B for all u ∈ R A (via substitution semantics). Then we can prove v u ∈ C B by a separate argument (SN + reachable values)?

Hmm, let me think about what general closure properties we need and prove them.

Actually, maybe a cleaner formulation of the whole logical relation avoids these nested application issues by using the "computational" definition:

`R T t := lc t ∧ SN t ∧ (∀ t', t --> t' → R T t')`?

That's circular (R appears in its own definition negatively). But it's a greatest fixed point; can't define by Fixpoint structurally. However, we could define it as "∀ n, R_n T t" where R_n is bounded-depth approximation! Since SN means all reduction sequences are finite (well-founded), maybe approximants indexed by n work: define R_n T t := lc t ∧ (all reduction sequences of length ≤ n lead to ... ) hmm. Actually with well-founded SN, we can define R by well-founded recursion on SN! But the definition itself is recursive on types with a nested SN recursion, which is difficult in Coq (needs well-founded recursion, definable but painful).

Alternative: define R T as an inductive predicate! For CBV:

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_Bool : forall t, lc t -> SN t -> R Ty_Bool t
| R_Arrow : forall T1 T2 t, lc t -> SN t -> (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u)) -> R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t
| R_expand : forall T t, lc t -> ... 
```

Hmm, with the inductive definition, we'd need expansion clauses for each type, and the induction principle would be by the inductive structure. The problem: adding an expansion clause "if all reducts are in R T then t ∈ R T" as an inductive constructor, plus proving inversion/closure. But the closure under reduction would need to be proven, and the induction principle with a "all reducts" constructor is not obviously well-founded (it's fine as an inductive definition as long as the constructor is monotone, but it might be too weak).

Let me step back and think about the actual structure of the standard proof. I recall the classic proof for CBV STLC SN in Coq by e.g. "Adequacy of a logical relation" or the "Tait" proof:

**Tait's method for CBV** (following Plotkin): Define the sets:
- `SN^T` (reducible terms of type T) as:
  - `SN^Bool := {t | SN t}` — actually needs to be "terms that are SN", 
  - `SN^{A→B} := {t | SN t ∧ ∀u. SN^A u → SN^B (t u)}`.
- and `SNv^T` (reducible values):
  - `SNv^Bool := {true, false}`,
  - `SNv^{A→B} := { abs A body | ∀u. SN^A u → SN^B (body·u) }`.

Then one proves:
- Values of reducible type are reducible terms? For Bool: true,false are SN ✓. For arrow: if v ∈ SNv^{A→B} then v ∈ SN^{A→B}: need SN v (vacuous for values? true/false/abs are SN ✓) and ∀u ∈ SN^A, v u ∈ SN^B — that's exactly the value condition. ✓
- Expansion: if t is SN and all its one-step reducts are in SN^T, then t ∈ SN^T. Is this true? For Bool: SN is given ✓ (no need for reducts). For arrow: need ∀u ∈ SN^A, t u ∈ SN^B. Hmm, this is the tricky bit again. But with the value/term separation and the right lemmas, there's a standard proof:

To show t u ∈ SN^B given SN t, SN u, and all reducts of t are in SN^{A→B}:
- Consider reducts of t u:
  * ST_AppAbs: t = abs A body, u value: reduct = open body u. Need open body u ∈ SN^B. Hmm! We know t ∈ SN^{A→B} (from... do we? We have all reducts of t are in R, but t itself might be a value/abstraction with no reducts). If t = abs, is t ∈ R^{A→B}? We need SN t (vacuous ✓) and ∀u ∈ SN^A, t u ∈ SN^B — which is what we're proving. Circular but can be resolved by well-founded induction on SN of t and u simultaneously? For t = abs, the term open body u is not a reduct of t, so the "all reducts" hypothesis gives nothing. Hmm.

So the expansion lemma for arrow types is false in general (take t = abs A body with body non-SN: t is SN vacuously and has no reducts, so vacuously all reducts in R, but t u --> open body u which may not be SN). So the expansion lemma can't be that simple. We need to use the typing hypothesis to know the body is good.

OK so let me restructure: the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case will prove the abstraction is in R by using the IH about the body, and the T_App case uses the arrow clause. So we don't need a general expansion lemma for abstractions. What we need is specific lemmas:

(a) R T is closed under step: R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'. [structural induction on T, uses lc and ST_App1]
(b) SN of application of a "good" abstraction: given R T1 u and ∀u' ∈ R T1, R T2 (open body u'), show R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u). [the L lemma above, proven how?]

Alternatively, define R in a way that makes the application case easier. Notice the general pattern: applications are "neutral" and their behavior is determined by the head. Standard approach: do the proof with a *value relation* and a *term relation*, where the term relation is defined as "SN and every reachable value is in the value relation" — this makes the beta case easier because:

`C T t := SN t ∧ ∀ t', t -->* t' → value t' → V T t'`.

Hmm, this is a reachability formulation. Then R-step closure is trivial. And for the beta case: C T (app (abs body) u) requires: SN (app (abs body) u) and every reachable value of app(abs body)u is in V T. So "every reachable value" needs knowledge about open body v, which we have: C T (open body v) gives SN and all reachable values are in V T. Then a reachable value from app(abs body)u is reachable from open body v (for some value v reachable from u) — need a "step-substitution" relation: if u -->* v then app(abs body)u -->* open body v? And C T (open body v) tells us those are in V T. This is manageable but requires multistep lemmas.

Let me compare with the direct lemma (b) approach and see if the nested application issue is really problematic.

**Alternative: mutual/stronger property with "application spine" generality.**

```
Good T t := lc t ∧ SN t ∧ ∀ n (args : list tm), (∀ i, R Ti (args i)) -> ...
```

**Better idea: Prove closure under "anti-reduction for application terms" as a separate lemma.**

**Lemma (app_anti_red)**: For all types T, terms t u:
  lc t -> lc u -> SN t -> SN u ->
  (∀ t', t --> t' -> R T (app t' u)) ->
  (∀ u', u --> u' -> R T (app t u')) ->
  (∀ t', t = tm_abs ... -> ...) hmm.

The problematic case is when the reduct comes from beta. Let's state it as:

For all t u, if (all one-step reducts of app t u are in R T) and lc (app t u) and SN (app t u), then R T (app t u), PROVIDED we handle the abstraction case. But as noted, when t = abs body and u is a value, the reduct open body u must be in R T, which is a hypothesis "all one-step reducts are in R T". So:

**Lemma (R_intro_by_reds)**: For all T, t: lc t → SN t → (∀ t', t --> t' → R T t') → R T t.
Counterexample from before: T = Bool, t = abs A Ω (open body non-SN): lc ✓, SN ✓, no reducts so third ✓, but R Bool t = SN t ✓!! Wait, R Bool t is just lc ∧ SN with my definition! There's no "value shape" requirement for Bool. So for Bool the lemma holds trivially.

For arrow T = A->B: R (A->B) t = lc t ∧ SN t ∧ ∀u ∈ R A, R B (t u). The counterexample: t = abs A Ω where Ω is non-SN. Then t ∈ R? We'd need ∀u ∈ R A, R B (t u). t u --> open Ω u which is non-SN, so R B (t u) fails. But the lemma's hypotheses would be satisfied (no reducts of t, SN vacuous). So the lemma fails for arrow.

BUT: in our use case, the third hypothesis "all reducts in R T" is only available for terms that actually have reducts; for abstractions it's vacuous. So we cannot derive R (A->B) of an arbitrary abstraction from reducts. However, in lemma (b), we're proving R T2 (app (abs body) u) — an application, not abstraction. Let's see if we can prove (b) by well-founded induction on (SN u) and (T2 structure) using an "anti-reduction for application terms" lemma that's valid.

Let me try to state and prove (b) for T2 = A->B directly with an inner SN-induction on u, and for the ∀v clause use... hmm, the ∀v clause needs R B of app (app (abs body) u) v.

Let's try proving R B (app (app (abs body) u) v) by induction on SN u *again*, with a suitable generalized property. Hmm, let's think about what the reducts of `app (app (abs body) u) v` are:
1. ST_App1 with inner reduct: if app (abs body) u --> t', then the whole --> app t' v. Inner reducts: (a) open body u when u value; (b) app (abs body) u' when u --> u'.
2. ST_AppAbs at the outer: requires the outer's function to be an abstraction: app (abs body) u is not an abstraction. ✗.
3. ST_App2 at the outer: requires value (app (abs body) u): never. ✗.

So reducts are: app (open body u) v and app (app (abs body) u') v.

Now:
- app (open body u) v: R B by hypothesis ∀u'∈R T1, R (A->B) (open body u') applied to u and v (need R T1 u ✓ and R A v ✓).
- app (app (abs body) u') v: by IH on SN u at the *same* goal type B? Hmm, what's the property we're proving by SN-induction? Let's define for fixed T2 = A->B:

P(u) := ∀ body, lc_at 1 body → (∀u'∈R T1, R (A->B) (open body u')) → R T1 u → R (A->B) (app (abs body) u).

We want P(u). SN-induction on u. To prove R (A->B) (app (abs body) u), we need its three components:
  - lc ✓
  - SN: prove by SN_intro: reducts are open body u (SN from R (A->B) (open body u)) and app (abs body) u' for u --> u'; use IH P(u') (since R T1 u' by closure) → gives R (A->B) (app (abs body) u') → SN ✓.
  - ∀v ∈ R A, R B (app (app (abs body) u) v): prove by induction on... the term app (app (abs body) u) v. Its reducts as analyzed; both in R B:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\ 
                      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Now, the fundamental theorem. Let's formalize.

Define substitution:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

Actually wait, is fvar substitution under bvar binders correct without shifting? The binder uses de Bruijn indices; substituting fvars doesn't affect indices, so yes.

We need an identity substitution for the closed case: `ids := fun x => tm_fvar x`, and show subst ids t = t. But for the theorem about closed terms, we can take sigma = ids and get subst ids t = t, and the context hypothesis is vacuous (empty Gamma = fun _ => None). R T t then gives SN t ✓.

Wait, but there's a subtlety: R T t requires locally_closed t, which we need. Hmm, the target theorem gives has_type empty t T; fundamental theorem with sigma = ids gives R T (subst ids t) = R T t, which includes SN t. But to get subst ids t = t we need a lemma. Even simpler: use sigma := fun x => tm_fvar x. Then subst sigma t = t by induction. ✓.

But hold on: the context hypothesis requires ∀x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x) = R T (fvar x). For Gamma = empty, vacuous ✓.

Now the T_Abs case in detail:

Typing: `T_Abs : forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)`.

Goal: ∀ sigma, ctx_ok Gamma sigma -> R (T1->T2) (subst sigma (abs T1 t1)) = R (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).

R (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) = lc (abs ...) ∧ SN (abs ...) ∧ ∀u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).

- lc: need lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). Hmm! We need locally_closed of the substitution instance, which requires sigma to map fvars to locally closed terms. But our hypothesis ctx_ok gives R T (sigma x) for Gamma x = Some T, which includes lc (sigma x) — but only for x in the domain of Gamma! What about fvars y not in Gamma (i.e., Gamma y = None)? In the body t1, there might be fvars not in Gamma? Well, for a well-typed term, all free fvars are in Gamma. Can we prove that? Typing gives that every fvar in t has a binding in Gamma. Hmm, that's a lemma: `has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, x free in t -> Gamma x <> None`. Hmm, or we can design the substitution semantics to only constrain the fvars that actually occur.

Alternatively, we can make ctx_ok stronger: ∀x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x), plus ∀x, Gamma x = None -> sigma x = fvar x (i.e., sigma is the identity outside Gamma). Then for a well-typed term, all free fvars are in Gamma, so all sigma images are lc. Hmm, but this requires the "free variables of typed terms are in the context" lemma.

Alternatively, define ctx_ok as: ∀x, (Gamma x = None -> sigma x = fvar x) ∧ (∀T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)). This "identity off-domain" condition is standard in locally-nameless substitution semantics (e.g., in Charguéraud's LN tutorial: "sigma is the identity on variables not in the context"). With this, to prove lc (subst sigma t) for a term t whose fvars are all in Gamma, we need a lemma "fv t ⊆ dom Gamma". And for the T_Var case, Gamma x = Some T gives R T (sigma x) ✓. For the T_Abs case: the body open t1 (fvar x) has fvars ⊆ dom (x:T1; Gamma) — from typing ✓ (including x itself). Then lc (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))) etc.

Hmm, so we need:
1. `fv` predicate: fvar occurrence.
2. `has_type Gamma t T -> fv t x -> Gamma x <> None` (or Gamma x = Some T').
3. Context extension: sigma' = x |-> u (i.e., update sigma x u) satisfies the extended context if u ∈ R T1 and sigma satisfies Gamma.
4. The substitution algebra: subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u when sigma' = sigma[x:=u] and x is not free in t1? Hmm, actually we need x fresh in t1? Let's think.

Standard LN: `open t (fvar x)` replaces bound var 0 with free var x. If we want to relate to `subst`:

subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) where sigma' x = u. Now, open t1 (fvar x) = open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1.

Lemma: subst sigma (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k (sigma x) (subst sigma t) — this holds for ALL sigma? Let's check: Subst and open_rec act on disjoint constructors, so they commute... but careful: sigma applied to the fvar x inserted by open_rec: on the left, the inserted fvar x is then substituted by sigma x. On the right, open_rec k (sigma x) inserts sigma x directly. And inside t, sigma substitutes fvars. But what if t contains fvar x itself (free occurrence)? Then on the left, that occurrence becomes sigma x; on the right too ✓. So the equality holds for all sigma. 

So subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma' t1) (sigma' x) = open (subst sigma' t1) u.

Now, if x is not free in t1 and x is not in the domain of Gamma... hmm, we want subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1. Since sigma' differs from sigma only at x, and x is not free in t1, we get subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1, provided... yes by a lemma: (∀y, fv-free y t -> sigma y = sigma' y) -> subst sigma t = subst sigma' t. Hmm, more precisely if x doesn't occur free in t1 and sigma' = update sigma x u, then subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1.

Actually, is x not free in t1? In the T_Abs rule, x ranges over atoms not in L, and L is an arbitrary list supplied by the rule. The standard convention: t1's free fvars are a finite set, so choose x not in L and not among t1's free vars. So yes, we can choose x fresh w.r.t. t1's free fvars. We need a lemma that there exists an atom not in a given list L and not free in t1: since atoms are nats, we can pick e.g. max(L) + sum of fv... or simpler: use a "pick fresh" lemma. In Coq, we can do: define `fv_list t` (list of atoms) or use a bound: `max_fv t + 1`. Alternatively, choose x := (sum of L) + (sum of fvs) + 1. Or use the classical fresh: `exists x, ~ In x L /\ ~ fv t x`. We can prove by taking x = S (max of list L and list of fvs). To avoid needing max over t's fvs, we can define a function `fv_bound t : nat` such that all free fvars of t are < fv_bound t. Then pick x = max(fv_bound t) (length L) + something.

Hmm, this is getting into real locally-nameless infrastructure. Let's think about how much we need to build:

Needed definitions:
- `subst : (atom -> tm) -> tm -> tm`.
- `fv : atom -> tm -> Prop` (or a boolean/list function).
- Identity-off-domain context condition.

Needed lemmas:
- subst identity/update lemmas:
  - `subst_id_on`: if ∀x, fv x t -> sigma x = sigma' x then subst sigma t = subst sigma' t.
  - `subst_update_eq`: subst (update_sigma x u) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst (update_sigma x u) t) u` — derived from the open/subst commutation.
  - `subst_open`: subst sigma (open t u) = open (subst sigma t) (subst sigma u). Wait, is this true in general? open t u = open_rec 0 u t, and subst commutes with open_rec: subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t) ✓ by induction on t (disjoint constructors). This doesn't require freshness. 
  - `subst_update_fresh`: if ~ fv x t and sigma' = update_sigma sigma x u, then subst sigma' t = subst sigma t. From subst_id_on.
- lc lemmas:
  - lc_at monotonicity in k: lc_at k t -> lc_at (k+1) t.
  - lc_at 0 t -> lc_at 0 (subst sigma t) given ∀x, lc_at 0 (sigma x). Actually need: if lc_at k t and ∀x, lc_at k (sigma x), then lc_at k (subst sigma t). Hmm, careful with binders: subst goes under binder without changing k, and sigma images are lc_at 0 (hence lc_at k by monotonicity). Actually we can prove: if lc_at k t and ∀x, lc_at 0 (sigma x), then lc_at k (subst sigma t). Under binder: lc_at (S k) body, IH gives lc_at (S k) (subst sigma body) using monotonicity of the sigma images to level S k ✓.
  - lc_at 0 (open t u) given lc_at 1 t and lc_at 0 u: standard.
  - lc_at preservation under substitution for fvars: `subst sigma t` lc if t lc and images lc.
  - `lc_open_subst`: given lc_at 1 t and lc u, lc (open t u) = lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 u t).
  - The lemma to recover lc_at 1 t from lc_at 0 (open t (fvar x)): earlier noted.
- typing lemmas:
  - `has_type_lc`: has_type Gamma t T -> locally_closed t.
  - `has_type_fv`: has_type Gamma t T -> fv x t -> exists T', Gamma x = Some T'. (Needed for ctx_ok to apply.)
- R lemmas:
  - `R_lc : R T t -> lc t`, `R_sn : R T t -> SN t`, `R_arrow_app : R (A->B) t -> R A u -> R B (t u)` — by unfolding.
  - `R_step : R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'`.
  - `anti_red_app`.
  - `abs_app_R`.
  - `R_subst`? For T_Abs we need to build R of subst instance. Hmm, actually the fundamental theorem handles it compositionally; we don't need a separate substitution lemma for R.

Let me now also think about whether we need `R_step` at all. In abs_app_R we need R T1 u' from R T1 u and u --> u' ✓. In anti_red_app, no. In the fundamental theorem, probably not. But R_step might be needed for the ST_App2/ST_App1 reduct cases... those are handled by anti_red_app hypotheses which come from the SN-induction. Let me double check abs_app_R's proof:

abs_app_R: hypotheses hbody : ∀u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u'), hR : R T1 u. Prove R T2 (app (abs body) u).
Proof by SN-induction on (R_sn hR : SN u). Hmm, in Coq, doing induction on an SN derivation while other hypotheses depend on u is tricky but manageable using the pattern:

```
assert (H : forall u, SN u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u)).
{ intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]. intros hR.
  apply anti_red_app.
  - (* lc *) ...
  - (* SN *) apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
    + (* ST_AppAbs *) apply R_sn, hbody, hR.
    + (* ST_App1: abs steps — contradiction *)
    + (* ST_App2 *) apply R_sn. apply IH. * assumption. ... hmm IH's form *)
  - (* all reducts *) intros t' Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
    + apply hbody, hR.
    + exfalso; ...
    + apply IH... 
}
```
The induction principle for SN: `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. When doing `induction Hsn`, Coq generates (for the inductive SN): given Hsn : SN u, in the case Hsn = SN_intro u Hreds, the IH is `forall t', u --> t' -> ...`? Hmm, the SN occurrence is in the premise: `forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'`. Induction on SN u: the case is SN_intro t H where H : forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'. The IH (from induction on the inductive predicate) applies to the SN arguments in H: `forall t', t --> t' -> P t'` where P is the motive. But the motive P depends on the SN argument, and we have an additional parameter (R T1 u). We need to generalize appropriately: P(sn_u) := R T1 u -> R T2 (app ... u) where u is determined by sn_u's index. Then IH : ∀ t', u --> t' -> (R T1 t' -> R T2 (app (abs body) t')). That's exactly what we need. But Coq's `induction Hsn` with a dependent hypothesis hR : R T1 u in context: we need to revert hR? But then the motive can't depend on hR. Let's see: goal is `R T2 (app (abs body) u)` with hR : R T1 u in context. `induction Hsn` — Coq tries motive `fun s => R T2 (app (abs body) u)` where u is fixed by s's index. Since Hsn : SN u, the index is u. Coq will want to generalize the goal over u, which appears in hR too; hR will need to be reverted, but hR's type depends on u, so we can revert hR, then induction, then intros. Standard.

Actually simpler: state a standalone lemma:
```
Lemma SN_app_abs : forall T1 T2 body u,
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u')) ->
  R T1 u ->
  R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 body u Hlc Hbody.
  (* prove by SN induction *)
  assert (Hgen : forall u, SN u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u)).
  { intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]. intros HR.
    apply anti_red_app.
    - (* lc *) ... uses R_lc HR, Hlc
    - (* SN *) apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. inversion Hs as [T t v Hlcabs Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hs1 Hlc2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv1 Hs2]; subst.
      + (* ST_AppAbs : t' = open t u *) apply R_sn, Hbody, HR.
      + (* ST_App1 : abs T1 body --> t1' *) exfalso. inversion Hs1.
      + (* ST_App2 : t' = app (abs T1 body) t2' *) apply R_sn. apply IH. assumption. (* HR for t2'? need R T1 t2' *)
        Hmm: IH : forall t', u --> t' -> R T1 t' -> R T2 (app (abs T1 body) t'). Wait the order: induction Hsn with motive includes HR. Let me define the motive to make IH : ∀t', u --> t' -> R T1 t' -> R T2 (...). So `apply IH; assumption` where the step premise Hs2 : u --> t2' and R T1 t2' via R_step HR Hs2.
      + ...
    - (* all reducts *) intros t' Hs. inversion Hs; subst; [apply Hbody, HR | exfalso; inversion ... | apply IH; [assumption | apply R_step with u; assumption]].
  }
  apply Hgen; [apply R_sn, H | assumption].
Qed.
```
Hmm, the induction hypothesis IH when using `induction Hsn` where the goal after intros is `R T1 u -> R T2 (...)`: Coq's induction will generalize dependent hypotheses automatically? No — `induction Hsn` will fail or produce a weak IH if hR : R T1 u is in the context depending on u. Actually, `induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]` when the context has `HR : R T1 u` and goal `R T2 (app (abs body) u)`: Coq will complain "cannot find a well-typed generalization" or will do the induction but leave HR un-generalized (which fails since u changes). The standard fix is `remember` or `revert HR`. But reverting HR defeats the motive (motive must not depend on HR, but it can depend on u). We can revert HR and then after induction intro it back: `revert HR. induction Hsn ... intros HR`. Wait, if HR : R T1 u is reverted, the goal becomes `R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs body) u)`, then induction on Hsn : SN u — motive `fun s => match s with SN u' => R T1 u' -> R T2 (app (abs body) u') end`? Coq computes the motive from the goal abstracting u (the index) which appears in the goal. So IH : ∀t', u --> t' -> R T1 t' -> R T2 (app (abs body) t'). 

Hmm, but Hsn appears in the context only as the thing we induct on; the goal after revert HR mentions u (from Hsn's index). Coq's induction on Hsn should abstract all occurrences of u in the goal, including the ones from Hsn's type? The goal is `R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs body) u)`. Coq will abstract u, giving motive `fun u0 => R T1 u0 -> R T2 (app (abs body) u0)` and case: `motive u` with the IH being `forall t', u --> t' -> motive t'`. Wait, the SN_intro constructor is `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. Inducting on `Hsn : SN u`: the case gives H : forall t', u --> t' -> SN t', and IH : forall t', u --> t' -> P (H t' ...)? Hmm, the induction scheme for this non-uniform inductive... SN is an inductive family with index u. The generated induction principle:

```
SN_ind : forall P : forall t, SN t -> Prop,
  (forall t (H : forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'), 
      (forall t' (Hs : t --> t'), P t' (H t' Hs)) -> P t (SN_intro t H)) ->
  forall t (s : SN t), P t s
```

If we do `induction Hsn`, Coq uses P t s := the goal with s replaced... Since our goal doesn't mention Hsn itself (only u), Coq's `induction` will produce motive P s := (match s with SN u' => R T1 u' -> ... end)? Actually Coq's `induction` on an indexed inductive with the index u appearing in the goal and in other hypotheses: it will clear/generalize. It might fail if u appears in other hypotheses (like `Hbody` doesn't mention u; `HR` does). Reverting HR first solves that. Then the goal `R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs body) u)` has u free; `induction Hsn` should abstract u and work. The IH will be `forall t' : tm, u --> t' -> R T1 t' -> R T2 (app (abs body) t')`. That's what we want. This pattern is standard for SN inductions; I've seen it work.

Hmm, one concern: Coq's `induction` on an inductive family where the index occurs in the goal — it generalizes dependent hypotheses. Since after revert HR, only u's occurrences are in the goal and Hsn, it should work.

Alright. Now let's think about the fundamental theorem.

```
Definition ctx_ok (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) /\
  (forall x, Gamma x = None -> sigma x = tm_fvar x).
```

Hmm, do we need the second condition? Let's see where it's used:
- T_Abs lc: need lc (subst sigma t1), i.e., lc_at 1 of the body's substitution. We know the body is... hmm, t1 is arbitrary but the opened version open t1 (fvar x) is in a typed context. We need lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). Hmm, we might derive lc of subst via lc_at 1 t1 plus images locally closed. Do we know lc_at 1 t1? From has_type_lc of the abs type? We'd need has_type Gamma (abs T1 t1) (T1->T2) -> lc_at 1 t1, which requires the "lc from open" lemma and the typing-lc lemma for the body. The typing-lc lemma for the body: has_type (x:T1;Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2 -> lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)) -> lc_at 1 t1 ✓ (that's the lemma we discussed). So lc_at 1 t1 is obtainable without knowing the fvars of t1 are in Gamma. But we also need lc of subst sigma t1, which needs the sigma images of *all fvars occurring in t1* to be lc. If t1 has an fvar y not in Gamma, then sigma y could be arbitrary (if we don't have the identity-off-domain condition). So we need either the identity-off-domain condition or the fv-in-context lemma.

Given has_type_fv (fv x t -> Gamma x ≠ None) and that typing of the abs gives typing of open t1 (fvar x) under the extended context... Do we know the fvars of t1 are in the extended context? The fvars of open t1 (fvar x) are the fvars of t1 plus x (if x occurs... well x might not occur, but the set of fvars of open t1 (fvar x) ⊆ fvs(t1) ∪ {x}). We'd need has_type_fv on the body: fv y (open t1 (fvar x)) -> (x:T1;Gamma) y ≠ None. Then for y ≠ x, Gamma y ≠ None (if update doesn't affect y). And for y occurring in t1: is fv y (open t1 (fvar x))? If y occurs in t1, then y occurs in open t1 (fvar x) unless the occurrence was... fvar occurrences are preserved by open_rec ✓. So fv y t1 -> fv y (open t1 (fvar x)) (assuming y ≠ x? if y = x, then x could occur in t1 but we choose x fresh so it doesn't). So we can derive: for y occurring in t1, Gamma y ≠ None, hence Gamma y = Some T for some T, and ctx_ok gives R T (sigma y) which includes lc (sigma y) ✓.

So with `has_type_fv`, we don't need the identity-off-domain condition. But wait, in the T_Var case, Gamma x = Some T and R T (sigma x) ✓. Fine.

Hmm, but there's another place: the substitution lemma for applying the IH in the T_Abs case. We define sigma' := update_sigma sigma x u where u ∈ R T1 u. We need:
(i) ctx_ok (update Gamma x T1) sigma': ∀y T, (x:T1;Gamma) y = Some T -> R T (sigma' y).
   - For y = x: sigma' x = u, and (x:T1;Gamma) x = Some T1 so T = T1, need R T1 u ✓.
   - For y ≠ x: (x:T1;Gamma) y = Gamma y = Some T, and sigma' y = sigma y, R T (sigma y) from ctx_ok ✓.

(ii) subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u.

So the choice of x must satisfy: x ∉ L (from the typing premise) and x not free in t1. Since L is a finite list and t1 has finitely many free vars, such x exists.

For finding x, I need an "atom fresh" construction. Options:
- Use a list of free vars of t1: define `fv_list : tm -> list atom`, then pick x = max of (L ++ fv_list t1) + 1. Need max function and "all elements < x" lemma. Since atoms are nats, we can define `bound t` such that all free fvars of t are < bound t (e.g., 1 + sum). Then x := max (S (bound t1)) (S (max_list L))... but need to handle empty list.

Simplest: define a function `fv_list : tm -> list atom` collecting free fvars. Then `fresh L t := S (fold_right max 0 (L ++ fv_list t))`. Prove:
- `fv_list_spec : forall t x, In x (fv_list t) <-> fv x t` (or just the direction needed: fv x t -> In x (fv_list t)).
- `le_max_list : In x l -> x <= fold_right max 0 l`.
Then fresh is not in L and not free in t.

Alternatively, avoid lists: define `fv_nat : tm -> nat` = 1 + sum of free var indices, then x := max ... Hmm, list approach is clean with Coq's List library (which is required already: `From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List`).

Actually, even simpler: choose x = `S (sum (L ++ fv_list t1))`. With a lemma that every element of a list is ≤ sum of the list. That's easy by induction:
```
Lemma In_le_sum l x : In x l -> x <= fold_right plus 0 l.
```
But sum could be 0 for empty lists; with S it's > 0 ✓. And any element ≤ sum < S(sum) ✓. And if the element appears in l, then x_elem ≤ sum(l) < S(sum(l)) so x_elem ≠ S(sum(l)) and also not In? No! Careful: we need to show the chosen fresh atom is NOT in the list. If y ∈ l then y ≤ sum(l) < sum(l)+1 = chosen; but the chosen atom could equal some element z ∉ l with z = chosen. We need: chosen ∉ L, i.e., no element of L equals chosen. Since every element of L is ≤ sum(L++fv) = sum < chosen, no element equals chosen ✓. Similarly for fv_list. Good.

So: `fresh (L : list atom) (t : tm) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.add 0 (L ++ fv_list t))`.
- `fresh_not_in_L : ~ In (fresh L t) L`.
- `fresh_not_fv : ~ fv (fresh L t) t`.

**Alternative: avoid substitution by working with contexts of fvars and a "value environment" that is itself a context mapping to fvars?** Hmm, the issue: R T1 u quantification needs arbitrary u.

**Alternative: use the "closing substitution" only with fvars and a Kripke-style relation?** The standard "semantic typing" for locally nameless in SF's `Stlc` uses `Gamma |= t \in T` defined directly on open terms with a context of types, via a recursive definition using "forall x not in L, ..." pattern. Let me recall how SF does `STLC` metatheory with locally nameless... In SF `Stlc.v`, typing is on locally nameless terms and semantics is given by a *closed* relation on terms with free variables, using "empty context" only for closed terms. Hmm.

Honestly, the substitution approach is standard and I've done similar proofs. Let's also consider a completely different, possibly simpler approach: **normalization by evaluation / Hereditary substitutions**? Probably more work.

**Another alternative: Strong normalization via translation to a known SN system?** E.g., prove SN using the "reducibility" but with a much simpler relation: `R T t := SN t` at Bool, and at arrow use the "hereditary" quantification. That's what we have.

Let me also consider: since the term language only has functions and booleans, maybe we can define SN via a simple measure? Like "number of beta redexes" or a graded size? For STLC CBV, there's no simple measure; logical relations are the way.

OK, let's now be very careful about the precise R definition and all lemmas, then write them.

### Detailed plan

Definitions:
```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
Hmm, note: Coq's Fixpoint requires structural recursion on T; R appears applied to T1 and T2 (subterms) ✓. But the definition of R is not a Fixpoint returning a function? It can be: `Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop := match T with ... end.` Then R T1 u is fine. Need to make sure `locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t` etc. typecheck.

Actually, since R's definition unfolds by type, for proving lemmas I'll use `simpl` or explicit rewriting. Let me define R as a Fixpoint on T returning `tm -> Prop`:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
                            (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
The inner `R T1 u` refers to the recursively defined function; since T1 is a subterm, this is accepted (nested recursion at structurally smaller arguments is fine for Fixpoint? Coq allows recursive calls on subterms of the matched argument, even in a function body being defined ✓).

Lemmas:
```coq
Lemma R_lc : forall T t, R T t -> locally_closed t.
Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Lemma R_app : forall T1 T2 t u, R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u).
```
These by destruct T; simpl; tauto.

```coq
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
```
Induction on T. For Bool: R Bool t = lc t ∧ SN t; need lc t' and SN t'. lc preservation under step; SN closure under step.
For Arrow T1 T2: R = lc ∧ SN ∧ (∀u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u)). Need:
 - lc t' (from lc t and step) ✓
 - SN t' ✓
 - ∀u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app t' u). Have R T2 (app t u). Since t --> t', app t u --> app t' u by ST_App1 with locally_closed u (from R_lc). Then apply IH for T2: `R_step T2 (app t u) (app t' u)`. ✓ (Recursive call on T2 which is a subterm of T ✓ structural induction.)

So need:
- `step_lc_preserve : lc t -> t --> t' -> lc t'`.
- `SN_step : SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'` (standard: inversion on SN; SN_intro with the reducts).

Let me prove SN_step: 
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hsn Hstep. remember Hsn as Hsn' eqn:E. (* or induction Hsn *)
  induction Hsn as [t Hreds IH].
  apply Hreds. assumption.
Qed.
```
Wait: induction on Hsn : SN t. Case SN_intro t Hreds IH where Hreds : ∀t', t --> t' -> SN t'. Goal SN t'. Apply Hreds ✓. But the goal t' varies; with Hsn in the context. Use `induction Hsn` and then `apply Hreds; assumption`. Since the induction's motive is `fun s => forall t', step (the term of s) t' -> SN t'`? Hmm, better state it as:
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t, SN t -> forall t', step t t' -> SN t'.
Proof. intros t Hsn. induction Hsn as [t Hreds IH]. intros t' Hs. apply Hreds. assumption. Qed.
```
The IH isn't even needed since Hreds directly gives it. Actually in the SN_intro case, the goal after intros is SN t' and Hreds : ∀t', t --> t' -> SN t' gives it ✓.

- `lc_step_preserve`: by induction/inversion on the step:
  - ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T body) v, t' = open body v; need lc (open body v) from lc (abs body) i.e. lc_at 1 body and lc v. Lemma: lc_at 0 u -> lc_at 1 body -> lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 u body). Generalize: lc_at k u -> lc_at (S k) body -> lc_at k (open_rec k u body)? Hmm, let's define the standard: `lc_open_rec : lc_at (S k) body -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u body)`. Check t = bvar i: body = bvar i, lc_at (S k) (bvar i) → i < S k, i.e., i ≤ k. open_rec k u (bvar i): if i = k then u (lc_at k ✓), else bvar i with i < k < ... need i < k for lc_at k (bvar i): from i < S k and i ≠ k → i < k ✓. Binder case: body = abs T s, lc_at (S k) (abs T s) → lc_at (S (S k)) s; open_rec k u (abs T s) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u s); need lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u s): use IH with k' := S k: lc_at (S (S k)) s ✓ and lc_at (S k) u from lc_at k u by monotonicity ✓. Good. So need monotonicity of lc_at.
  - ST_App1: t = app t1 t2, t' = app t1' t2, with lc t1, lc t2, t1 --> t1'; IH gives lc t1' ✓.
  - ST_App2: similar.
  So `lc_step_preserve` by induction on the step derivation (or inversion) using IH.

  Actually since step is an inductive relation, `induction Hstep` works with the hypotheses lc t.

- `lc_at_mono : lc_at k t -> lc_at (k + n) t` or specifically `lc_at k t -> lc_at (S k) t`. Prove by induction on t or on the lc_at derivation. Then anti_red_app. Let me write its proof carefully.

```coq
Lemma anti_red_app : forall T (t u : tm),
  locally_closed (tm_app t u) ->
  strongly_normalizing (tm_app t u) ->
  (forall s, step (tm_app t u) s -> R T s) ->
  R T (tm_app t u).
Proof.
  intros T. induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; intros t u Hlc Hsn Hreds.
  - (* Bool *) simpl. split; assumption.
  - (* Arrow *) simpl. split; [assumption|]. split; [assumption|].
    intros v Hv.
    (* Goal: R T2 (tm_app (tm_app t u) v) *)
    apply IH2.  (* anti_red_app at type T2 with t' := tm_app t u, u' := v *)
    + (* lc (tm_app (tm_app t u) v) *) simpl in *. 
      ...
    + (* SN (tm_app (tm_app t u) v) *)
      ...
    + (* all reducts *)
      intros s Hs. inversion Hs as [...]; subst.
      * (* ST_AppAbs: function = abs *) exfalso; (* tm_app t u is an application *) ...
      * (* ST_App1: tm_app t u --> s1 *) apply Hreds in H. (* H : R (T1->T2) s1 *) 
        apply R_app in H; [..]. (* R T2 (tm_app s1 v) *) 
        Hmm, need R (T1->T2) s1 and R T1 v.
      * (* ST_App2: value (tm_app t u) *) exfalso; inversion on value.
Qed.
```
Wait, careful with the inversion of step: the step relation's constructors:
- ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, locally_closed (tm_abs T t) -> value v -> step (tm_app (tm_abs T t) v) (open t v)
- ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, step t1 t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> step (tm_app t1 t2) (tm_app t1' t2)
- ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> step t2 t2' -> step (tm_app v1 t2) (tm_app v1 t2')

To invert `step (tm_app (tm_app t u) v) s`:
- ST_AppAbs requires `tm_app (tm_app t u) v = tm_app (tm_abs T' body) v'`, so the function `tm_app t u` = `tm_abs T' body` — impossible by no-confusion (tm_app vs tm_abs constructors differ).
- ST_App1: function = `tm_app t u`, so we get `step (tm_app t u) t1'` and s = `tm_app t1' v`.
- ST_App2: requires `value (tm_app t u)` — impossible since value has no app constructor.

In Coq, `inversion Hs` with the unification should handle cases and leave impossible ones as goals with absurd equalities, which `discriminate` handles. Good.

For the "SN (app (app t u) v)" part:
```
apply SN_intro. intros s Hs.
inversion Hs as [...]; subst.
- (* ST_AppAbs *) exfalso; discriminate/... 
- (* ST_App1 *) apply R_sn. apply R_app with (T1 := T1); [apply Hreds; assumption | assumption].
- (* ST_App2 *) exfalso; (* value (tm_app t u) *) inversion H.
```
Hmm wait: in ST_App1 case for the outer app: premises `step (tm_app t u) s1` and `locally_closed v`, conclusion s = tm_app s1 v. We need SN (tm_app s1 v). We have Hreds : R T (tm_app t u) → applied to step gives R (T1->T2) s1; then R_app gives R T2 (tm_app s1 v); R_sn gives SN ✓.

Note: Here T = T1->T2, and R (T1->T2) s1 = lc ∧ SN ∧ ∀v, R T1 v -> R T2 (s1 v). Apply to Hv : R T1 v ✓.

Now `abs_app_R`:

```coq
Lemma R_app_abs : forall T1 T2 body u,
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u')) ->
  R T1 u ->
  R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 body u Hbody_lc Hbody HR.
  assert (Hgen : forall u, strongly_normalizing u -> R T1 u ->
                 R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u)).
  { intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]. intros HRu.
    apply anti_red_app.
    - (* lc *) ...: need lc (app (abs body) u): lc_at 0 (tm_abs T1 body) from Hbody_lc, lc u from R_lc HRu.
    - (* SN *) apply SN_intro. intros s Hs.
      inversion Hs as [T t v Hlc Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hstep Hlc2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv Hstep]; subst.
      + (* ST_AppAbs: s = open body u *) apply R_sn, Hbody, HRu.
      + (* ST_App1: abs body --> t1' *) exfalso. inversion Hstep.  (* abs doesn't step *)
      + (* ST_App2: s = app (abs T1 body) t2', with u --> t2' *) 
        apply R_sn. apply IH. assumption. apply R_step with u; assumption.
        (* IH : forall t', u --> t' -> R T1 t' -> R T2 (app (abs body) t')? *)
    - (* all reducts *) intros s Hs.
      inversion Hs as [...]; subst.
      + apply Hbody, HRu.
      + exfalso; inversion Hstep.
      + apply IH; assumption + R_step.
  }
  apply Hgen; [apply R_sn, HR | assumption].
Qed.
```

Concern: `induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]` where the goal is `R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs body) u)` with HRu : R T1 u in the context. As discussed, need `revert HRu` or use the pattern `intros u Hsn; induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]; intros HRu`. But at that point, the context has HRu : R T1 u. If I write `intros u Hsn`, then Hsn : SN u and HRu : R T1 u remains from the outer intros... Let me structure: in the assert, do `intros u Hsn; revert Hsn; ...` hmm.

Better: state Hgen as a separate top-level lemma or use this pattern:
```
assert (Hgen : forall u, SN u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u)).
{ intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH]. intros HRu. ... }
```
Here after `intros u Hsn`, Coq's context has HR? No: HR is the outer hypothesis (R T1 u for the *specific* u, but we've shadowed u by intro-ing a new u). Since `intros u` shadows the outer u, HR : R T1 (original u) doesn't refer to the new u — but it's still in the context with the same name "H R"? The outer hypothesis was named HR and refers to the original u. When we `intros u`, the new u shadows the old name but HR's type still uses the *original* u. Then `induction Hsn` where Hsn : SN u(new) — HR doesn't mention the new u, so it's fine! Then `intros HRu` gives R T1 u(new). ✓. But wait, Coq's induction might still try to generalize HR (since its type mentions `u` — but does it? HR : R T1 u_original, and the name `u` in the goal refers to u_new. Coq uses the actual term, not names. Since HR's type mentions the original u variable (a different variable), it doesn't depend on u_new ✓).

But careful: the outer hypothesis HR was introduced as `intros ... HR` for the lemma's u. If I shadow, name conflicts are OK.

Alternatively, cleaner: prove a general lemma separately:

```coq
Lemma SN_app_abs_gen : forall T1 T2 body,
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u')) ->
  forall u, strongly_normalizing u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).

  intros T1 T2 body Hlc Hbody u Hsn.
  induction Hsn as [u Hreds IH].
  intros HR.
  ...
Qed.
```
Here the induction on Hsn with goal `R T1 u -> R T2 (...)`: Coq will need to abstract u from the goal; Hreds/IH are fine. Since no other hypothesis mentions u, this works cleanly.

Then:
```coq
Lemma R_app_abs : forall T1 T2 body u, lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u')) -> R T1 u ->
  R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).
Proof. intros. eapply SN_app_abs_gen; eauto using R_sn. Qed.
```

Now, the fundamental theorem. Let's define:

```coq
Definition ctx_sat (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x).
```

Hmm, do we need the "identity off-domain"? Let me re-examine. In the T_Abs lc argument, we inferred lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1) from lc of the body instance — this required that `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))` equals `open (subst sigma t1) u`, which required subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1 (x fresh not in t1) ✓ — no need for off-domain identity. And lc of the body instance comes from R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))) which we have from the IH. So we don't need to know anything about sigma on fvars not in Gamma! Because the only substituted terms appearing are for fvars occurring in terms, and typing gives those are in Gamma... 

Hmm wait, but actually in T_Abs, considering a general sigma: the term subst sigma t1 might mention sigma y for y free in t1. For the lc argument, I used the equality with the body instance where y is free in t1 → y free in open t1 (fvar x) → Gamma y = Some T (by has_type_fv) → R T (sigma y) → lc ✓. Wait, but I need to be careful: `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u` requires subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1. This uses only x not free in t1. But `subst sigma t1` involves sigma y for all y free in t1 — those are fine (they're exactly what appears in the body instance too, since fv y (open t1 (fvar x)) holds for y free in t1) ✓.

So actually the equality `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u` is fine and its lc gives lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1) via the open-inversion lemma, without needing to know lc of individual sigma images! Because we know lc_at 0 of the whole body instance including sigma y's. But for the lc of `subst sigma t1` at level 1 we need... the inversion lemma gives lc_at 1 t from lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 u t), where t = subst sigma t1 here. And lc u is needed? Let me recheck the inversion lemma: `lc_open_inv : j <= k -> lc_at k (open_rec j u t) -> lc_at (S k) t` — no hypothesis on u! Interesting: because in the bvar j case, the result is u, and we need lc_at (S k) (bvar j) which holds since j ≤ k < S k. So indeed no requirement on u. 

So we get lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1) for free from R T2 (body instance) — no has_type_fv needed?! Hmm, wait: the equality subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u requires subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1, which requires x ∉ fv t1. Fine. And subst sigma' (fvar x) = u ✓. So yes! It seems we might not need the identity-off-domain condition nor has_type_fv at all!

Let me double check other places where ctx_sat (just the domain condition) is used:
- T_Var: Gamma x = Some T → R T (sigma x) ✓.
- T_App: IH gives R (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1) and R T1 (subst sigma t2), then R_app ✓.
- T_True/False: R Bool tm_true = lc ∧ SN ✓ (both hold). Need lc true and SN true ✓.
- T_Abs: as described.

So the context interpretation only needs the domain condition. 

Now, one more check: in T_Abs, to prove R (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)), I need:
  lc (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) = lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1) — proven via the body instance and inversion ✓.
  SN (abs ...) — vacuous.
  ∀u, R T1 u → R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u) — via R_app_abs, whose hypotheses are lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1) ✓ (just proven) and ∀u'∈R T1, R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u') — proven via the IH and the equality ✓.

Wait, but hold on: is `subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)` definitionally `tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)`? With my subst definition, yes ✓.

And open body u where body := subst sigma t1: we need R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u). From the IH: R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))), and we rewrite via the equality. But which equality? We need:
  subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u.
Proof: subst sigma' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) 
     = open_rec 0 (subst sigma' (fvar x)) (subst sigma' t1)   [subst_open_rec, requiring all sigma' images lc!]
     = open_rec 0 u (subst sigma' t1)
     = open_rec 0 u (subst sigma t1).   [x fresh]

Hmm, so `subst_open_rec` requires that all sigma' images are locally closed. Do we know that? sigma' images: for y = x, u which is in R T1 → lc ✓; for y ≠ x, sigma y. Do we know lc (sigma y) for all y? Not from ctx_sat alone! ctx_sat only gives R (Gamma y) (sigma y) for y ∈ dom(Gamma). For y ∉ dom(Gamma), sigma y is unconstrained and could be non-lc.

Hmm. But wait: does the equality actually require all images lc, or only the ones actually used? The lemma `subst_open_rec` as I formulated needs all images to be lc (because the free bvars of sigma y could be affected by opening at any level). Hmm. But maybe we only need lc for fvars occurring in t1 — those are in Gamma and hence lc. Let's formulate a more refined lemma:

`subst_open_rec` with hypothesis: for all y, fv y t → lc_at 0 (sigma y), then subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t). Hmm, u itself: on the left, the inserted term is u then substituted? Let's recompute the identity: LHS = subst sigma (open_rec k u t). At the position where open_rec inserts u, the substitution gives subst sigma u. RHS = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t). So the identity should hold if sigma's images (for fvars in t) are lc, AND we need to consider that on the RHS, open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t) might affect bvars inside substituted sigma y images at the right level. If all sigma y (for y in t) are lc, then at any level they're unaffected ✓. Also for the subtree u: LHS has subst sigma u; RHS inserts subst sigma u ✓ same.

But also: subst sigma u appears, and it's the same on both sides ✓.

What about the sigma images of fvars inside... u? On the LHS, open_rec k u t inserts u, then subst sigma substitutes fvars in u — giving subst sigma u. On the RHS, open_rec k (subst sigma u) inserts subst sigma u ✓. Fine.

So the refined lemma: if ∀y, fv y t -> lc (sigma y), then subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t). Hmm, but wait: is it also affected by the need that u's free bvars...? On the LHS, u is inserted at depth k inside t; then subst sigma recursively processes u along with the surrounding binders? No! subst sigma (open_rec k u t): subst processes the term open_rec k u t. Inside that term, u appears at depth d+k for some d (binders above the insertion point in t). subst doesn't care about depth (it only replaces fvars), so it just recursively substitutes in u: result subst sigma u placed at that depth. On the RHS, open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t): inserts subst sigma u at the corresponding depth, and also processes (subst sigma t) for openings. The sigma images: at a fvar y in t at depth d, LHS gives sigma y (a subtree at depth d); RHS gives open_rec (k+d) (subst sigma u) (sigma y). If sigma y is lc, no replacement ✓. So the refined hypothesis suffices.

In our case t = t1, and fv y t1 → y ∈ dom(Gamma) → R (Gamma y) (sigma y) → lc ✓. 

So we need `has_type_fv` and the fact that the body typing `(x:T1;Gamma) |- open t1 (fvar x) : T2` implies for y free in t1, Gamma y ≠ None. Let me prove `has_type_fv : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, fv x t -> exists T', Gamma x = Some T'`.

Induction on has_type:
- T_Var: Gamma x0 = Some T; t = fvar x0; fv x (fvar x0) means x = x0 → exists T ✓.
- T_Abs: t = abs T1 t1; fv x (abs ...) → fv x t1. Premise: ∀x0 ∉ L, (x0:T1;Gamma) |- open t1 (fvar x0) : T2. Pick x0 fresh w.r.t. L and x? We need Gamma x = Some T'. From IH applied to the body with fvar x: is fv x (open t1 (fvar x0))? If x ≠ x0, then fv x t1 → fv x (open t1 (fvar x0))? Hmm, need x0 ≠ x (choose fresh). Also need x0 ∉ L. Then IH gives (x0:T1;Gamma) x = Some T'. Since x ≠ x0, this equals Gamma x = Some T' ✓. Need a lemma: fv x t1 → x ≠ x0 → fv x (open_rec 0 (fvar x0) t1)? Hmm, is that true? open_rec replaces bvar 0 with fvar x0. The fvar x occurrences in t1 remain. Under binders, still remain. So yes, fv is preserved by open_rec when... let me think: fv x (open_rec k u t) ← fv x t, provided fv x u? For our case, if fv x t1 then fv x (open_rec 0 (fvar x0) t1) unless all occurrences of x in t1 are... no, the fvar x nodes are never removed by open_rec ✓. So a simple lemma: fv x t -> fv x (open_rec k u t). Prove by induction on t.
  And we need to pick x0 fresh: exists x0, x0 ∉ L ∧ x0 ≠ x. Easy with fresh.
- T_App: fv x (app t1 t2) → fv x t1 ∨ fv x t2; use IH ✓.

Actually, for an inductive family `Inductive SN : tm -> Prop := SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', ...) -> SN t.` — when we `induction Hsn`, Coq substitutes t := u and introduces the hypotheses. Simplest: avoid `as` patterns: `induction Hsn.` then use the auto-named hypotheses (H, IHHsn or similar), or use `induction Hsn as [u' Hreds IH]` and test. Alternatively, define a custom lemma:

```
Lemma SN_ind' : forall (P : tm -> Prop),
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t.
Proof.
  intros P H t Hsn. induction Hsn as [t Hreds IH]. apply H. intros t' Hs. apply IH. assumption.
Qed.
```
This gives a clean induction principle where P can be any Prop (not depending on the SN derivation), which is what we want! Then in SN_app_abs_gen, we'd need P(u) := R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u) but the SN hypothesis is on u. So:

```
intros T1 T2 body Hlc Hbody u Hsn.
revert u Hsn.  (* or generalize *)
induction Hsn using SN_ind'? 
```

```

```

```
Lemma SN_app_abs_gen : forall T1 T2 body,
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u')) ->
  forall u, SN u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).

  (* goal: forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> R T1 t' -> R T2 (app (abs body) t')) -> R T1 t -> R T2 (app (abs body) t) *)

  ...

```
Wait: `revert u Hsn` makes the goal `forall u, SN u -> ...`? No: after `intros T1 T2 body Hlc Hbody u Hsn`, goal is `R T1 u -> R T2 (...)`. Reverting u and Hsn gives `forall u, SN u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (...)`. Then `refine (SN_ind' P _)` where the goal must match `SN_ind' P : forall t, SN t -> P t`, i.e., P := fun u => R T1 u -> R T2 (...). Applying SN_ind' to the goal `forall u, SN u -> R T1 u -> R T2 ...` gives the subgoal `forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t` ✓. 

Actually, I realize `revert u Hsn` isn't needed if I write the lemma statement as `forall u, SN u -> ...` and prove with `intros ... u Hsn; revert Hsn; refine (SN_ind' ...)`. Hmm, whatever. Let me just carefully write it.

Hmm, also: is `SN_ind'` provable? `induction Hsn as [t Hreds IH]` then `apply H; intros t' Hs; apply IH; assumption` — the IH should be `forall t', t --> t' -> P t'`? With P not depending on the SN proof, yes: the motive is fun t _ => P t, and the IH is ∀t' (Hs : t --> t'), P t'. Actually, wait: the induction principle for SN as an inductive family:

```
SN_ind : forall P : forall t : tm, SN t -> Prop,
         (forall (t : tm) (H : forall t' : tm, step t t' -> SN t'),
             (forall (t' : tm) (H0 : step t t'), P t' (H t' H0)) ->
             P t (SN_intro t H)) ->
         forall (t : tm) (s : SN t), P t s.
```
If we set P t s := P' t (constant in s), we get SN_ind' as a derived lemma. Proving it directly:
```
Lemma SN_ind' : forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t.
Proof.
  intros P Hstep t Hsn.
  induction Hsn as [t' Hreds IH].
  apply Hstep. intros t'' Hs. apply IH. assumption.
Qed.
```
Does `induction Hsn` here work? The goal after intros is P t. Hsn : SN t. The induction motive: fun t s => P t (s ignored); Coq should handle it since P t doesn't mention Hsn. The case: t = t', Hreds : ∀t'', step t' t'' -> SN t'', and IH : ∀t'' (Hs : step t' t''), P t''. Apply Hstep: need ∀t'' (step t' t''), P t''; intros; apply IH; assumption ✓. I'll test in Coq.

Alternatively I can prove SN_app_abs_gen by well-founded induction using the "SN_intro" structure directly. The SN_ind' approach is clean.

Now the `subst`:
```coq
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition ids : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.

Lemma subst_ids : forall t, subst ids t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; f_equal; auto. Qed.

Definition update_sigma (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

Lemma subst_update_same : forall sigma x u t,
  ~ fv x t -> subst (update_sigma sigma x u) t = subst sigma t.
```

Need `fv`. Let me define fv as a Fixpoint returning Prop or bool. I'll use Prop:
```coq
Fixpoint fv (x : atom) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => False
  | tm_fvar y => x = y
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv x t1 \/ fv x t2
  | tm_abs T t1 => fv x t1
  | tm_true => False
  | tm_false => False
  end.
```
Then an induction on t with `~ fv x t` works.

Actually for picking fresh x w.r.t. t's free vars, a list is easier: define `fv_list : tm -> list atom` and relate: `fv x t -> In x (fv_list t)`. Alternatively, define fresh based on a bound function. Let me do:

```coq
Fixpoint fv_list (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => nil
  | tm_fvar y => y :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv_list t1 ++ fv_list t2
  | tm_abs T t1 => fv_list t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Lemma fv_fv_list : forall t x, fv x t -> In x (fv_list t).
Proof. induction t; simpl; intros; auto; 
  try (destruct H; [apply in_or_app; left | apply in_or_app; right]; auto).
Qed.
```
Hmm, but I need `fv` and `fv_list` consistent. For `~ fv x t`, I get it from `~ In x (fv_list t)` if fresh is not in fv_list. So:

```coq
Definition fresh (L : list atom) (t : tm) : atom :=
  S (fold_right Nat.add 0 (L ++ fv_list t)).

Lemma In_fold_right_add_le : forall l x, In x l -> x <= fold_right Nat.add 0 l.
Proof.
  induction l; simpl; intros; [contradiction|].
  destruct H.
  - subst. lia.
  - pose proof (IHl H). lia.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin_L : forall L t, ~ In (fresh L t) L.
Proof.
  intros L t Hin. unfold fresh in Hin.
  apply in_or_app in Hin; apply In_fold_right_add_le in Hin. lia.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_fv : forall L t x, fv x t -> x <> fresh L t.
(* or ~ fv (fresh L t) t *)
Lemma fresh_not_fv : forall L t, ~ fv (fresh L t) t.
Proof.
  intros L t Hfv. apply fv_fv_list in Hfv. unfold fresh in Hfv.
  apply in_or_app in Hfv; apply In_fold_right_add_le in Hfv; lia.
Qed.
```

Good.

```coq
Lemma subst_update_same : forall sigma x u t,
  ~ fv x t -> subst (update_sigma sigma x u) t = subst sigma t.

  - (* abs *) rewrite IHt; auto. destruct H; auto.
Qed.
```

```coq
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma t k u,

  subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).
```

Now, the R lemmas. Let me define R:

```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Note: `R` as a Fixpoint returning a function. When we write `R T u`, it's `(R T) u` ✓. In the definition, `R T1 u` refers to the recursive call `(R T1) u` ✓.

Potential issue: Coq might complain "not structurally recursive" because R T1 is applied to u — no, that's fine: we're calling R on T1 which is a structural subterm. ✓.

Lemmas:
```coq
Lemma R_lc : forall T t, R T t -> locally_closed t.
```

```
Lemma R_app : forall T1 T2 t u, R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t -> R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 t u H Hu. unfold R in H. fold R in H. (* hmm *)
```
Since R is a Fixpoint, `unfold R in H` might not work directly (Fixpoint unfold: `unfold R` replaces R with its body, then the recursive occurrences remain R). Actually `unfold R in H` should beta-reduce: R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t → locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u)). Let me test.
